## docs/testing/imported-pq-reality.md

# Imported entries with a paired PQ REALITY final

This continuation admits the existing `reality-pq-vision` runtime as a paired
**Local** final for the imported entry families already admitted to ordinary
`reality-vision`. It uses the existing in-process native Xray transport and
retains the original imported entry, paired final, credentials and independent
Start Layers. It adds no logical mode, external engine process or second system
VPN.

## Exact scope

| Imported label | Concrete wire variants in this gate |
| --- | --- |
| `socks5` | Authenticated SOCKS5 |
| `shadowsocks` | Captured Shadowsocks 2022 |
| `hysteria2` | Captured Hysteria2 |
| `http-connect` | HTTP CONNECT |
| `https-connect` | TLS-authenticated HTTPS CONNECT |
| `wireguard` | Captured WireGuard |
| `amneziawg` | Captured AmneziaWG |
| `openvpn` | TCP and UDP, independently captured |

The eight labels give nine wire variants. Each has three entry Start Layer
choices and three final Start Layer choices: Off, AES-256-GCM and
AES-256-GCM with XOR whitening. The separate PQ inventory therefore contains
**81 graphs per producer**. These entries use the same exact compiler and
capture/restore boundaries as the corresponding ordinary REALITY graphs.

The final must retain its original PQ VLESS encryption, UUID, REALITY settings,
Vision flow and complete native asset map. An ordinary asset map relabeled as
PQ, missing encryption or assets, a foreign layer/route, or a different final
mode is refused. DNS and the paired final identity/token remain owned by the
final hop. The original Vision UDP443 policy remains explicit: selecting PQ
does not grant an otherwise forbidden UDP443 flow or encrypted-DNS bootstrap.

Android's service admission now recognizes the exact
`multihop-imported-reality-pq-vision` session ID. Android and Apple builders,
selectors and temporary SpeedLab forms preserve the selected PQ final.
Desktop capture, retained launch and dynamic catalogs include the exact final;
the daily and temporary catalog contract requires the complete seven-final
set, including all six previously supported finals.

## Evidence layers

| Gate | What a passing gate proves | What remains outside that evidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shared compiler | 81 exact captures, 144 source/downgrade refusals, and 90 selected-DNS/flow cases | A running network session |
| Actual Android JVM producer | Shipping Java capture, staging, restore and service admission against the real shared Go compiler; 81 exported receipts | Android VPN permission, JNI packaging and device traffic |
| Actual Apple Swift producer | Shipping Swift capture, selection and restoration against the real shared Go compiler; 81 exported graphs | Network Extension entitlement and device traffic |
| Desktop producer | Actual retained capture/launch, final DNS, physical-owner preservation, restore and source refusal; 81 exported graphs | A privileged installed native helper or OS TUN |
| Pinned native parser | All 324 graphs from four independent producers construct and close through the actual Libbox `CheckConfig` registry | Started transport, public egress or device acceptance |
| Completed/reused native bundle | All 81 desktop PQ graphs pass the actual matching bundle's `sing-box check -c`, with unchanged graph bytes and bundle receipt | Protected public startup, started transport or installed-device acceptance |
| Pinned encrypted loopback | Real native ML-KEM matching-key traffic, wrong-key refusal and restored-key traffic; TCP/UDP and retained connection-owner regressions | Imported physical entry routing or an OS TUN |
| Imported physical peers | Original imported provider, owned physical tuple, independent layers, final DNS/identity and failure/cancellation controls | Production router, signed mobile application or installed device acceptance |

The parser checks the complete separate filenames and binds each name to its
actual transport, OpenVPN TCP/UDP choice, CONNECT TLS policy and both Start
Layers. Five native negative controls copy valid graphs under incompatible
names and require rejection at those exact boundaries. Duplicate or renamed
valid graphs cannot satisfy the inventory. Missing, extra, linked, skipped,
failed or duplicate evidence is rejected.

Observed host verification in this continuation includes Android's 12,034
positive assertions across all 81 PQ captures, all six causal mutations
(including the old service allowlist), Apple's 2,078 PQ assertions and 407
isolated selector assertions, and desktop's 127 race-enabled verdicts across
four roots. The native parser passed 328 verdicts for the 324 graphs plus the
five renamed-source controls. The encrypted loopback passed 14 race-enabled
verdicts across three roots, including actual wrong-ML-KEM-key refusal and
restoration. Readiness detector controls are checked independently.

These counts refer to the bounded continuation runs. Each native gate emits
its own source/binary fingerprint; the counts are not an acceptance receipt for
every later rebased commit or release head. A complete native bundle and its
verified Rust archive were unavailable locally, so the completed/reused bundle
gate has modeled orchestration checks here and requires actual matching-host
CI execution.

These are bounded source, host-producer and reference results. Hosted physical
peer acceptance, installed desktop/mobile behavior and live router acceptance
must be tracked separately. A local interface/Netlink denial must remain a
failed physical attempt; it must never be converted into a skipped or synthetic
passing route proof.

The local imported-peer image compiled, but the physical gate failed. An
isolated original `socks5/off/off/success` witness identified the actual
boundary: `route ip+net: netlinkrib: operation not permitted`. The physical
lookup and protection checks remain mandatory in the hosted gate. This local
result supplies no physical imported-peer or packet-entry acceptance.

## Reproduction and required CI

Use Go 1.26.3 and the exact prepared desktop core. The preparers and gates bind
the sing-box revision `1ac1a339cb1223e9c70eae14c44411c75033c02d` and Xray revision
`50231eaff98ccc31b5cbd247a721c16e97fe5ec1`, check projected source bytes and record
binary/source hashes. The parser's extra reader is a declared test-only Go
overlay; no mobile runtime or replacement production source is installed by
the gate.

Create separate empty directories for each producer. With an actual JDK and
Android JSON implementation, export Android's nine explicit PQ phases:

```sh
ROUTERVPN_ANDROID_IMPORTED_PQ_XRAY_FIXTURES="$ANDROID_PQ" \
python3 android/test_android_imported_router.py \
  --phase pq-reality--socks5 --phase pq-reality--shadowsocks \
  --phase pq-reality--hysteria2 --phase pq-reality--http-connect \
  --phase pq-reality--https-connect --phase pq-reality--wireguard \
  --phase pq-reality--amneziawg --phase pq-reality--openvpn-tcp \
  --phase pq-reality--openvpn-udp
```

Export Apple with a working Swift 6/Foundation toolchain and desktop through
the shipping Go test package:

```sh
ROUTERVPN_APPLE_IMPORTED_PQ_XRAY_FIXTURES="$APPLE_PQ" \
  python3 deploy/test_ios_tor_profile.py
ROUTERVPN_DESKTOP_IMPORTED_PQ_XRAY_FIXTURES="$DESKTOP_PQ" \
  go test -race ./cmd/client -run '^TestDesktopImportedPQReality' -count=1 -timeout=180s
```

Then require all host inventories in the native parser, followed by the
separate encrypted loopback and physical imported-peer gates:

```sh
python3 deploy/test_imported_pq_workflow.py
python3 deploy/test_mobile_imported_pq_xray_runner.py
python3 deploy/test_mobile_imported_pq_xray.py "$CORE" \
  --android "$ANDROID_PQ" --apple "$APPLE_PQ" --desktop "$DESKTOP_PQ"
python3 deploy/test_mobile_imported_pq_xray.py "$CORE" --loopback-only
python3 deploy/test_mobile_imported_pq_xray.py "$CORE" --reference-only
```

The reusable `imported-pq-reality-native.yml` workflow requires the actual Swift
artifact and all nine Android phases plus desktop exports before parsing all
four inventories. The focused ordinary REALITY workflow retains its original
gates and then calls this separate PQ workflow. Release-candidate aggregation
also requires it. Native Linux builds retain the ordinary gates and add the
separate PQ contract, parser, loopback and physical-peer stages. PQ files and
counts never replace or satisfy an ordinary REALITY export set.

`build-native-runtime.py` also runs `verify_imported_desktop_pq_configs` after
both fresh completion and reuse on each matching Windows, macOS and Linux
x64/ARM64 host. This separate gate requires all 82 original desktop producer
verdicts, keeps all 81 emitted graphs unchanged while invoking the completed
bundle's actual executable, and revalidates the full bundle receipt afterward.
Existing ordinary, packet and OpenVPN bundle inventories remain mandatory.

## Remaining boundaries

Protected desktop startup and imported-source replay through the public native
command/Box path remain a separate implementation dependency at this unit's
evidence boundary. The desktop client marks imported launches with
`HOMEVPN_NODE_PHYSICAL_REQUIRED=1`, and the Windows helper invokes
`routervpn-node`; the corresponding imported native factory, source replay and
physical-policy admission are being integrated separately. This unit's desktop
results establish the producer, shared graph, native registry and protocol
extension. They do not establish a fully working protected desktop launch or
replace that native helper integration and its acceptance tests.

This unit does not complete imported **Server** or **Compare/Auto** execution,
imported XHTTP/composite finals, arbitrary hop graphs, or all original project
requirements. Unsupported combinations remain refused by the compiler.
Physical/device acceptance requires its own exact-source evidence, and live
router or production deployment still requires separate authorization.

## docs/testing/imported-xhttp.md

# Imported entries with a paired XHTTP REALITY final

This continuation admits the existing `reality-xhttp` mode as a paired **Local**
final through the original imported entry. Android, Apple and desktop use the
existing shared graph compiler and in-process native Xray transport. The daily
and temporary selectors expose this exact final; desktop catalogs now require
eight finals, retaining the seven previously supported choices.

## Exact graph and policy scope

The separate XHTTP inventory contains nine concrete imported transports:
SOCKS5, Shadowsocks 2022, Hysteria2, HTTP CONNECT, HTTPS CONNECT, WireGuard,
AmneziaWG, OpenVPN TCP and OpenVPN UDP. Each transport has three entry Start
Layer choices and three independent final Start Layer choices: Off,
AES-256-GCM, and AES-256-GCM with XOR whitening. This produces **81 graphs per
producer**, with four independent producers supplying **324 native parses**.

Capture and restore retain the original imported credentials, paired final,
API/token identity, selected final DNS, MTU/protection selection, source
receipt, and both Start Layers. The final's native graph retains its complete
user settings, hybrid `mlkem768x25519plus.` encryption, REALITY settings,
XHTTP path/settings and FinalMask. Only the existing compiler's owned local
listener and final-layer endpoint adaptation is allowed. A changed path or
FinalMask under an old source receipt fails validation.

Admission remains exact. `reality-xhttp` requires an absent or empty VLESS
flow and the existing `xhttp` or `splithttp` network spelling. Its settings
require an absolute path and `auto` mode. Raw TCP, Vision flows, alternate
download endpoints, foreign settings, ordinary encryption and relabeled PQ
Vision assets are refused. The existing raw `xray.json` asset form remains
supported; absence of an optional `sing-box.json` wrapper alone is not an
error. A present malformed wrapper is refused.

The existing pinned Xray implementation chooses stream-one for auto XHTTP
with REALITY. Its server checks the normalized path prefix, so the live
negative control uses a disjoint path rather than a child of the valid path.
The empty-flow XHTTP path can carry UDP/443, including selected HTTP/3 DNS.
The ordinary and PQ Vision flow restrictions remain unchanged. Resolver
ownership, hostname bootstrap, certificate/SNI checks and the original caller
deadline still apply; adding XHTTP does not authorize a direct resolver path.

The same connection owner must complete the original public path proof,
selected final DNS proof and paired final identity proof before Connected. A failed
proof, missing DNS capability, cancellation, close, owner replacement or
network change cannot leave the captured path healthy or reusable. Server,
Compare/Auto and arbitrary additional hops remain outside this extension.

## Evidence boundaries

| Gate | What it executes | What it does not establish |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shared compiler and retained controller | Exact independent graphs, downgrade/source refusals, selected-DNS policies, real HTTP proof endpoints and cancellation/owner controls | The controller fixture models its native engine and physical observation |
| Android producer | Shipping Java capture, service admission, staging and restoration through the real shared Go compiler; 81 original receipts | Android VPN permission, packaged JNI or device traffic |
| Apple producer | Shipping Swift capture, selection and restoration through the real shared Go compiler; 81 original graphs | Network Extension entitlement, packaged framework or device traffic |
| Desktop producer | Actual capture and retained launch graph, final DNS, physical-owner preservation, MTU restoration and source refusal | A privileged installed helper or OS TUN |
| Native parser | All four producer inventories through the pinned Libbox `CheckConfig` registry, with exact graph/name binding | A started transport or public egress |
| Encrypted protocol loopback | Matching traffic, wrong disjoint path and wrong valid ML-KEM key refusal, restored traffic, selected DNS and empty-flow UDP/443 | Physical imported-provider routing or an OS TUN |
| Imported physical peers | Original imported provider, frozen physical tuple, both real layers, final DNS/identity and cancellation/failure controls | A production router or signed installed application |
| Completed/reused native bundle | All 81 desktop graphs checked by the matching completed bundle executable, with original bytes and receipt revalidated | Protected public startup or device acceptance |

The native parser requires every separate original file once. It binds the
filename to the actual imported transport, CONNECT TLS policy, OpenVPN wire
transport and both layer axes. Five controls substitute valid graphs under
incompatible names and require refusal by the compiled native reader. Missing,
extra, duplicate, linked, skipped or failed evidence never satisfies an
inventory. XHTTP files cannot replace an ordinary or PQ inventory.

The encrypted refusal controls require traffic to reach the original owned
underlay and inspect fresh diagnostics from the same encrypted peer. Wrong
path must produce that peer's path-validation refusal, and wrong ML-KEM key
must produce its ML-KEM handshake failure. Matching traffic is restored after
each fault. Malformed key encoding separately fails native startup before an
outer socket opens. These checks use the existing caller deadlines, without
retrying under a renewed budget.

Host producer verification for this continuation includes all 81 Android
graphs and 13,262 assertions, six rejected causal mutations, all 81 Apple
graphs and 3,201 assertions, and the isolated Apple selector's 407 assertions.
Those exports preceded the clean rebase onto the published readiness and
selector repairs; the frozen producer behavior and captured bytes are checked
again by the final native parser. Their counts do not claim a packaged mobile
run or acceptance of every later commit.

The final frozen source passed 833 race-enabled shared verdicts across 14
roots, including 452 XHTTP verdicts and the retained ordinary/PQ boundaries.
Desktop passed 145 race-enabled verdicts across four roots, including its
81-graph capture and actual daily/temporary catalog checks. The final native
parser passed all 324 graphs, giving 328 verdicts across four roots, and all
five renamed-graph controls were rejected. The encrypted loopback passed all
32 verdicts across three roots, with the three readiness-detector controls
also passing. Parser and loopback retained the same source and prepared-source
receipts throughout. Each native stage emits its exact source, prepared-source
and executable fingerprints.

No local physical imported-peer or installed-device acceptance is claimed.
An interface/Netlink denial remains a failed physical attempt. No fallback,
skip, simulated route or relaxed protection check can turn it into acceptance.
The final-source imported-peer package compiled with both XHTTP roots present.
Its original `socks5/off/off/success` witness failed at
`route ip+net: netlinkrib: operation not permitted`; the source and executable
receipts stayed unchanged. This is a blocked physical witness, not a passing
reference matrix.
The completed/reused bundle orchestration has executable modeled checks for
all six matching Windows/macOS/Linux x64/ARM64 hosts; actual completed-bundle
acceptance remains a matching-host CI gate.

## Reproduction and mandatory CI

Use Go 1.26.3 and the exact prepared desktop core. The preparers pin sing-box
`1ac1a339cb1223e9c70eae14c44411c75033c02d` and Xray
`50231eaff98ccc31b5cbd247a721c16e97fe5ec1`, verify projected source bytes and
retain the original physical guards. Use separate empty export directories.

```sh
ROUTERVPN_ANDROID_IMPORTED_XHTTP_XRAY_FIXTURES="$ANDROID_XHTTP" \
python3 android/test_android_imported_router.py \
  --phase xhttp--socks5 --phase xhttp--shadowsocks \
  --phase xhttp--hysteria2 --phase xhttp--http-connect \
  --phase xhttp--https-connect --phase xhttp--wireguard \
  --phase xhttp--amneziawg --phase xhttp--openvpn-tcp \
  --phase xhttp--openvpn-udp
ROUTERVPN_APPLE_IMPORTED_XHTTP_XRAY_FIXTURES="$APPLE_XHTTP" \
  python3 deploy/test_ios_tor_profile.py
ROUTERVPN_DESKTOP_IMPORTED_XHTTP_XRAY_FIXTURES="$DESKTOP_XHTTP" \
  go test -race ./cmd/client -run '^TestDesktopImportedXHTTP' -count=1 -timeout=180s

python3 deploy/test_imported_xhttp_workflow.py
python3 deploy/test_mobile_imported_xhttp_xray_runner.py
python3 deploy/test_mobile_imported_xhttp_xray.py "$CORE" \
  --android "$ANDROID_XHTTP" --apple "$APPLE_XHTTP" --desktop "$DESKTOP_XHTTP"
python3 deploy/test_mobile_imported_xhttp_xray.py "$CORE" --loopback-only
python3 deploy/test_mobile_imported_xhttp_xray.py "$CORE" --reference-only
```

`imported-xhttp-native.yml` is a reusable mandatory workflow. Its Apple job
uploads the actual Swift exports; its Linux job requires that artifact, all
nine Android phases and the actual desktop exports before parsing all four
inventories. Separate loopback and physical reference stages must then pass.
The focused workflow retains its ordinary and PQ jobs and adds the XHTTP job.
Release aggregation independently requires the XHTTP workflow; the actual
aggregate checker rejects removed, substituted or conditional dependencies.

Matching Linux native builds require the separate XHTTP contract, parser,
loopback and physical reference stages after the original gates.
`verify_imported_desktop_xhttp_configs` also runs after both fresh completion
and reuse on every matching desktop host. It requires all 82 original
producer verdicts, exactly 81 unchanged files and the same completed bundle
receipt before and after actual `sing-box check -c` execution. The ordinary
26-file, packet 108-file, OpenVPN 108-file and PQ 81-file bundle inventories,
their verdicts and their existing deadlines remain mandatory.

## docs/testing/linux-imported-policy-lifecycle.md

# Linux imported WireGuard entry and paired Shadowsocks lifecycle

This unit adds the sealed compiler and retained native preparation for one
protected Local pair: an independently imported WireGuard entry and a paired
Router VPN Shadowsocks final, with both Start Layers off. Other Linux imported
pairs and layers remain rejected by this compiler boundary.

The policy retains the original imported controller. The derived source binds
the target, session, complete entry/final configuration, independent identities,
selected final DNS, both proof listeners, physical entry tuples and restored MTU.
It cannot be substituted for an ordinary NodeProfile. Linux and Windows source
identities differ even when they describe the same original pair and session.

The Linux native adapter shares the existing source admission and retirement
coordinator through the sealed physical-policy interface. It retains the actual
interface capture, exact socket broker, nftables baseline, guardian, original
parent callbacks and joined protection owner. An imported Box has its own typed
preparation and activation owner. Selected DNS is proved through that original
Box before activation returns. Public imported command and application admission
remain closed on Linux pending the authenticated parent integration and both
proofs. This prerequisite does not claim a usable protected Linux UI path.

## Required checks

- `TestDesktopImportedPolicyLinuxCapturedPair` checks complete graph derivation,
  physical entry ownership, selected final DNS, target binding and rejection of
  unintegrated entry/final/layer choices.
- `TestRouterVPNNodeLinuxLifecycleImportedCapturedPair` executes the generated
  compiler, native lower, interface capture, broker and lifecycle coordinator.
  Its privileged source constructor is modeled. Both on-connect and always
  policies run live, refused baseline, foreign compiler, refused admission,
  cancellation, changed baseline, partial construction and failed join cases.
- The existing desktop physical factory runner requires the test root and all
  sixteen leaves. The runner contract rejects missing, skipped and duplicate
  verdicts. These are part of the existing native source gate, not a separate
  optional workflow.

A sandbox that denies network-interface netlink reads cannot execute the strict
native fixture. A separate diagnostic interface model can check the remaining
coordinator logic, but does not replace a passing unmodified native-host run.
Kernel packet traffic, inherited-controller authentication, entry public proof,
paired-final authenticated proof, selected-DNS publication, cancellation,
restoration, MTU ownership and successor retirement require their full Linux
host integration before public admission is enabled.

## Observed local evidence

The shared imported-policy race selection passed in 29.253 seconds. The actual
pinned native desktop package and the generated Box package compiled. The
unmodified sixteen-case native fixture stopped at the environment's denied
`net.Interfaces` Netlink operation before its constructor; that is not a native
runtime pass.

A separate diagnostic-only copied-core overlay modeled interface enumeration
and readback. The final-source sixteen imported lifecycle cases passed under
race detection in 8.940 seconds; the existing and imported Linux lifecycle
regression selection passed in 27.370 seconds. The privileged constructor was
already an explicit fixture boundary. Neither interface overlay nor a test skip
was added to the repository. The existing Box prepared-owner check passed with
no interface overlay; it is not an imported end-to-end connection test.

The retained native constructor's exact nil/partial return boundary passed two
extracted race cases. Removing its nil-preservation check made the causal
control fail because a nil pointer became a retained nonnil interface. The
temporary harness was removed. Existing routing-runner checks and the exact
seventeen-verdict imported inventory controls passed; missing, skipped and
duplicate native results remain rejected.

## Authenticated imported child and final cover

The internal Linux imported child coordinator uses the same original inherited
FD 3 channel, FD 4 private directory and FD 5 image as the ordinary Linux child.
It authenticates those participants before reading `imported-source.json`,
recompiles its canonical imported constructor, compares the complete staged
configuration and binds both the command capture and control-selection hashes.
An ordinary `node-source.json` cannot fill the imported slot. This coordinator
remains unreachable from the public imported dispatcher until the Linux parent
host integration is ready.

Imported Linux cover is derived from the final service in that sealed graph.
The final node identity, private API, token, rate, packet/proof routing and source
digest remain captured. Readiness checks the selected final DNS, retained TUN,
MTU, transfer and exact cover count/digest. The child starts the actual captured
cover worker and retains its health in the existing heartbeat guard. It uses
the existing DNS query/cancel/result and MTU command loop.

The imported compiler remains alive until actual native source join and the
parent's retirement acknowledgment. Failed join or acknowledgment retains the
constructor and reports failure; neither cancellation nor an empty source
substitutes for that sequence.

The inherited-child gate includes both ordinary and imported variants of its
eleven boundaries: captured preparation, cancellation after admission, a
prepared but unstarted source, refused baseline, refused admission, cancellation
after sealed compile but before preparation, cancellation before compile,
changed configuration, changed selection, refused bootstrap and foreign image.
The pre-prepare fixture now establishes its real bootstrap and sealed control
before cancellation; it explicitly rejects retirement before empty-source join.
The earlier cancellation case requires compile rejection with no policy,
control, preparation or native owner. Production cancellation checks are unchanged.

The source cleanup harness executes the shipping join/retire functions and
existing cleanup loop with modeled callbacks. It rejects a causal mutation that
allows retirement before join. The native namespace cases still require a
matching privileged Linux runner; source/model results cannot replace them.

## docs/testing/llvm-release-asset-fallback.md

# Pinned LLVM release-asset download fallback

## Observed release blocker

The exact-main ARM64 auxiliary build at `63c6a935e9971ce28bd07688985178e7c17e4ef7`
failed in the pinned Windows Tor compiler prerequisite. Job
[129003139949](https://github.com/Eabusham2/router-vpn/actions/runs/42569431298/job/129003139949)
received HTTP 504 from the LLVM-MinGW release download before compiler execution.
Earlier exact-source runs reached the same download failure.

The installer now switches after a transient gateway response to the official
public GitHub binary asset API for the same pinned archive. The four asset IDs
were checked against the upstream `20261006` release metadata. This is a route
change within the existing three attempts and absolute 600-second download
budget. It adds no credentials, compiler version change, alternate archive,
checksum exception, or retry after a permanent HTTP error, transport failure,
partial download, cancellation, or integrity failure.

## Verification on 2026-11-09

- All 17 `VerifiedWindowsInstaller` tests passed, including the actual installer
  path with modeled primary gateway responses and a complete verified API body.
  The new cases cover API route/header identity, three-attempt exhaustion,
  permanent and transport errors, the original deadline, and refusal of long,
  short, changed, insecure, metadata, and partial API responses before extraction
  or compiler execution. Existing extraction, reuse, ownership, and compiler
  version checks remain enabled.
- An independent unauthenticated request downloaded the entire Linux AMD64 asset
  `618062377` from the official API. The redirect ended at
  `release-assets.githubusercontent.com`, with HTTP 200, 84,003,044 bytes, and
  SHA-256 `5f9c6ed95b2d4bdb2869a488c5fd5857fbdabcf288a0aa3eb1da43f6a08d8ab4`.
  Both values match the existing pin. The download took 10.23 seconds; this
  inspection did not execute the archive.

These results verify the route and installer invariants. A later hosted build
must establish successful compiler execution and release publication at its own
commit; the repaired downloader alone does not establish either result.

References: [upstream release](https://github.com/mstorsjo/llvm-mingw/releases/tag/20261006)
and [official release-asset API documentation](https://docs.github.com/en/rest/releases/assets#get-a-release-asset).

## docs/testing/measurement-admission-after-stop.md

# Measurement admission after Stop

## Observed failure and cause

The complete source-security job `128939429684` on
`587ebb69b7ebbdfbeec061ff8e8d86831d51a45e` reported
`TestDesktopTorOrdinaryStartupLastAndCompleteSetupRetainExactEntry/hysteria2`
failing after 0.16 seconds. Immediately before that assertion, the controller
logged that Startup Last was skipped because another connection or settings
transaction was active. Restoration had not entered its native startup path.
The separate ten-minute package alarm is documented in
[the complete-suite budget guide](go-source-contract-budget.md).

A successful Tor Connect already commits the saved Last choice, rechecks the
captured native proof and sets Connected before returning. The asynchronous
selected-DNS observer can still be finishing its own result. Ordinary Stop
retires the runtime, while that observer rejects obsolete results through its
captured session and path binding. Previously, measurement adoption acquired
the general transaction lock before validating that binding. A result arriving
after Stop could therefore reserve the lock briefly, then discover it belonged
to the retired session. An immediate replacement request could be refused
during that interval.

## Source correction

`beginAsyncMeasurementAdoption` now reserves its transaction through an
asynchronous-measurement-specific admission helper. The helper holds the
captured tracker and application locks, checks controller retirement and the
existing complete session/path binding, then attempts `operationMu.TryLock`
inside that same critical section. This reservation never waits for another
transaction while holding the tracker/application locks.

Those two locks are released before the existing protection-recovery and
via-entry cleanup guards run. Both guards receive the original measurement
context. Adoption still reacquires its original locks and performs the full
final validation before exposing a result to the caller. The compatibility
`beginNodeBoundOperation` helper and other callers retain their existing
behavior.

## Bounded evidence

The regression test creates the real selected-DNS path context, holds its
tracker against periodic invalidation, executes `stopModeWithIntent` under the
connection transaction, and then submits the old captured result with the
application owner held. It observes either a context-aware wait before
reservation or actual transaction ownership by the old implementation.
Unmodified source failed the reservation assertion in **0.01 seconds**; its
package finished in **0.061 seconds**. This is a captured-DNS adoption control
with a real Stop state transition, not a native-device test.

Additional controls exercise current and stale protection bootstrap admission,
retained Off and via-entry guards, cancellation before reservation, cancellation
inside an admitted guard, and an entered recovery callback whose cleanup is
held after cancellation. The entered callback must retain the transaction
until cleanup joins and retain its exact failed recovery receipt afterward.
The modeled native read callback must remain uncalled when bootstrap fails.

The final focused controller gate passed **120 verdicts across 24 roots**
under the race detector, with no failure or skip; the package ran in
**3.740 seconds**. The backend session transaction audit and all **seven**
selected-DNS gate contracts also passed. The command is:

```sh
go test -race -json ./cmd/client \
  -run '^(TestStoppedDNS|TestAsyncMeasurementAdoption|TestStaleMeasurement|TestMeasurement(Adoption|Observer|TorObservation|OwnedNodeObservation)|TestLiveMeasurementsCannotPersistAcrossAnotherTransaction|TestSessionDNSWorker|TestDNSProofPath|TestDesktopNodeRecoveryContext)' \
  -count=1 -timeout=5m
python3 deploy/backend-session-transaction-audit.py
python3 deploy/test_recovered_dns_proof_gate.py
```

The original eight-protocol Tor lifecycle root was also attempted unchanged.
Every local leaf stopped at its initial Connect, with `Tor preparation could
not capture its physical network`, before the restoration assertion. The full
attempt is retained as an unavailable local integration boundary. The existing
hosted test remains required; local admission controls do not establish its
native restoration result.

## Remaining lifecycle boundary

This correction prevents an obsolete result from reserving a replacement
connection transaction or entering another owner's protection recovery. It
does not implement a new nonterminal selected-DNS cancel-and-join phase inside
ordinary Stop. The reusable observer, its original deadlines and periodic
cancellation remain unchanged. Terminal controller shutdown still uses its
separate DNS-worker retirement and join path. A broader reusable DNS drain
requires its own lifecycle design and evidence.

## docs/testing/mtu-finalization-rollback.md

# MTU final adoption and checked rollback

The shared MTU controller can measure and select a new TUN size before a
platform's retained native lease accepts its final commit. If that final
adoption fails, the controller must not keep the candidate configuration while
the native owner has already restored the original interface. A later Retest
would otherwise compare the original interface against the failed candidate's
configuration.

`FinalizationRollbackOwner` is an optional, observation-only contract. It uses
the failed operation's existing cleanup authority and cannot renew its deadline
or submit another mutation. The returned session, path and MTU must match the
original pre-run capture. The controller separately captures the current
interface again, validates the complete pre-run configuration, and restores
that exact configuration only if both observations agree. Failure remains a
failure; the result is restored and unmeasured. The final controller mutex
admission checks the same cleanup context again, so waiting for admission cannot
extend the authority of an earlier observation.

The executable test runs the real shared selection and finalization path with
controlled platform TUN readers. It measures a distinct winner, rejects final
adoption, restores the original owned reader, and then runs a second Retest
using the restored configuration. Negative cases reject another session, path,
reader or MTU, an unconfirmed or cancelled rollback, and replacement after the
first rollback observation.

Validation used Go 1.26.3 on Linux:

```sh
go test -json -race ./internal/mobilemtu -count=3
```

All 61 root tests and 152 verdicts passed per iteration, including the eight
finalization rollback cases. After adding the final admission check, a focused
race run of `^TestFinalizedRollback` passed two root tests and ten verdicts. The
additional causal test holds the actual final admission mutex, closes the
original cleanup authority after the live proof/configuration validation, and
then releases admission; no restoration may be published.

The fixtures exercise shared policy, selection, cancellation and configuration
ownership. They do not establish physical interface operation or packaged native
application acceptance.


---

## docs/testing/naive-h2-transport.md

# Native Naive H2 transport ownership

The pinned Cronet runtime can request a UDP socket while starting an H2
connection, even with QUIC disabled. The H2 outbound now rejects that request
before it reaches the retained lower dialer. Application datagrams continue
through the original UoT-v2 TCP stream. H3 retains the exact original dialer.

## Original failure and cause

Two isolated Linux/AMD64 native executions failed the original no-lower-UDP
assertion in all eight cases. The second execution retained the assertion and
recorded the exact request: `udp [2001:4860:4860::8888]:443`. The lower fixture
refused it before network I/O. Valid credentials still carried real H2 TCP and
UoT-v2 payloads; wrong username, password, SNI and certificate refused payload.
Those payload observations did not turn the original failed witness into a pass.

Both the pinned Cronet wrapper and library identify native submodule
`SagerNet/naiveproxy` revision `72a06c9fca0e2d228588c7f3074bf7efff3ff686`.
Its [original host resolver source](https://github.com/SagerNet/naiveproxy/blob/72a06c9fca0e2d228588c7f3074bf7efff3ff686/src/net/dns/host_resolver_manager.cc)
uses that IPv6 address in `StartIPv6ReachabilityCheck`. It creates a datagram
socket, connects it to port 443 and inspects the local address. This is separate
from a selected DNS query and has no authority to use an H2 lower UDP transport.

## Declared shipping change

`deploy/native_naive_transport_policy.py` verifies sing-box revision
`1ac1a339cb1223e9c70eae14c44411c75033c02d` and the complete original Naive/UoT
source inventory. It changes one Cronet option in `protocol/naive/outbound.go`:
the dialer becomes `routerVPNNaiveTransport(outboundDialer, options.QUIC)`.
The separate DNS resolver closure still captures the original `ResolveDialer`,
including its selected transport and query options.

For H2, the helper forwards canonical TCP calls with their original context,
destination, connection and error. All other `DialContext` networks and every
`ListenPacket` call fail before lower I/O. For H3, the helper returns the original
dialer object and preserves TCP, UDP and packet-listener behavior.

Mobile Libbox, relay/server and standalone desktop preparation all apply this
policy. Their input closures include the original policy and tests. Mobile and
desktop native source checks require the exact declared output. Preparation
validates the entire plan before writing, refuses source drift and linked or
shared output owners, and supports idempotent preparation and read-only
verification. The native runner requires all 13 transport-policy verdicts and
keeps the original physical-bypass and H3-downgrade controls.

No selected DNS transport, bootstrap dependency, socket grant, operation budget,
or imported-H2 admission changes in this unit.

## Executed evidence

The fixed run completed on 2026-11-09 at 03:30:11 UTC. It used the unchanged
original failing witness, SHA256
`5ac6523a24d0ef6f50473a9096290146bd71483fded9678f780446f51e505fb5`, with its
unchanged 90-second runtime limit. The Linux/AMD64 Cronet library was
11,915,768 bytes with SHA256
`23109c55b08829bb1a68682a61a64852b1bbf243753b153cbd8f6e4ea2c05294`.

- The actual core policy passed 13 race-enabled verdicts across H2 and H3.
- Three executable causal controls failed at the intended policy assertions:
  unwrapped H2, admitted H2 datagram dialing and wrapped H3.
- The unchanged real native witness passed its root and eight leaves. Every
  leaf recorded zero lower UDP calls. Valid literal IPv4 endpoints with a
  different TLS SNI carried authenticated TCP and UoT-v2 UDP payloads. Both
  captured UDP and TCP bootstrap metadata were retained without DNS queries.
- Wrong user, password, SNI and certificate prevented authenticated payload.
  A deliberate native-only hostname mutation still made four calls through
  the original DNS resolver closure and reached neither lower TCP nor the TLS
  peer. The last positive case owns a fresh peer.
- Source, prepared-byte, witness and native-library identities were verified
  before and after execution. The fixed image SHA256 was
  `d67a84b11eeb24eb34baddd8e01d8b79c643d3a5c843c208d61cb9fe27c3139d`.

The frozen execution was based on `fb924d4849b448d519c573a78e021b595a216007`.
After preserving the Tor caller and ordinary Stop changes through
`b16656f53faf55f4a59e7c6f1f2f3ac7cee506cc`, the complete prepared source
comparison confirmed unchanged compiled native components. Only runner and
contract-test receipt entries changed. All 59 Unix and 46 Windows production
negative controls still construct from the exact prepared source, including
both original Naive controls. Existing native baseline, routing, Windows scope
and orchestration contracts also passed.

This evidence establishes the H2 transport boundary. It does not establish
imported-H2 graph acceptance, a real network bootstrap exchange, encrypted H3
peer acceptance, TUN/firewall activation, device acceptance or release status.
The literal endpoint witness does not remove the deliberately retained
bootstrap dependency or authorize UDP bootstrap through a TCP-only entry.

## docs/testing/native-dns-measurement-clock.md

# Checked native DNS measurement clock adoption

The shared `internal/dnsmeasure` sample path now captures
`hopmeasure.StartInterval` before issuing either the selected native DNS query
or a public comparison query. Windows therefore uses the checked performance
counter already owned by native hop measurements; other targets retain their
monotonic clock. Query deadlines still use the original caller's bounded
context. A clock initialization failure stops the sample before DNS work.

Both paths read that same interval after a complete answer and the existing
native identity validation. Read failures retain their original error cause.
Zero, negative and nonfinite results cannot become a score, and positive
fractional milliseconds are not rounded or raised to an invented minimum.
The caller and captured engine identity are checked again after clock readback.
The original public socket still closes and joins its cancellation watcher
before a sample returns; a close failure continues to invalidate the sample.

The clock factory is a private, per-call test seam. Production always supplies
`startProbeInterval`; there is no mutable clock override on a service or in
package state, no alternate resolver and no wall-clock timing fallback.

## Native source projection

Mobile preparation already relocates both `dnsmeasure` and `hopmeasure` through
its declared shared package list. Desktop's later DNS copy now applies its
existing declared import relocation as well, so it cannot overwrite that copy
with an import of the parent repository's internal package. The independent
SOCKS/REALITY source checker permits exactly the new `hopmeasure` namespace
relocation and continues comparing every other source byte.

## Verification

With Go 1.26.3 on Linux:

- `go test -race -count=1 ./internal/dnsmeasure ./internal/hopmeasure` passed.
  This includes actual framed TCP, TLS and HTTP DNS exchanges, answer matching,
  cancellation, native owner changes, admission, draining and close failures.
- The DNS clock tests retain the original zero/backward-clock and
  nanosecond/submillisecond precision cases, now through complete selected and
  public sample paths. They additionally cover nonfinite results, initialization
  and read failures, original error causes, exact one-time clock capture/read,
  socket closure, and cancellation or owner replacement during clock readback.
- Two disposable Go overlays provided causal controls. Replacing interval
  readback with a fabricated 1 ms value failed 20 sample cases. Removing the
  final owner/caller check failed all four selected/public cancellation and
  replacement cases. The overlays did not alter repository source or tests.
- The actual desktop relocation/write functions copied both packages into a
  disposable native module namespace. Every DNS copy matched the independent
  import-only transformation; both relocated package race suites passed.
- The DNS package and tests cross-compiled for Windows AMD64. This establishes
  compilation, not Windows performance-counter execution.
- Both edited Python files passed `py_compile`; `git diff --check` passed.

## Remaining evidence and scope

This is shared native DNS timing adoption. It does not complete the remaining
client telemetry/throughput clock adoption, other multihop capability gaps,
full pinned-native build execution, current-SHA release packages, or physical
selected-path and network-transition acceptance. The disposable namespace run
is not labeled as a full native-core or device run. Matching-host Windows
counter execution and the normal complete native preparation gates remain
required in their existing release workflows.

## docs/testing/native-layer-paired-listeners.md

# Native Start Layer paired listener allocation

An exact native packet-reference run (`42410240765`, job `128518270528`,
source `92678123`) exhausted all three paired-listener attempts after the
UDP-selected ephemeral ports each collided with an existing TCP listener.
Choosing a UDP port does not reserve its TCP counterpart.

On Unix, the native layer fixture now asks TCP for port zero first and retains
that exact listener while binding UDP at its returned endpoint. If the UDP
counterpart collides, it closes only the captured partial owner and retries
within the unchanged three-attempt bound. Fatal errors, malformed returned
endpoints, and close failures remain failures. Both listener owners remain
captured until the complete pair joins cleanup. Windows retains its distinct
random-candidate policy and existing native address-in-use/permission handling.

The executable paired-listener inventory keeps all twelve prior allocation
controls, requires the production dispatch case, and adds ten Unix cases for
held TCP listeners, real UDP collisions and bounded exhaustion, fatal reads,
foreign endpoints, and exact partial-close failures. The production control
rejects the old dispatch. A second source counterfactual restores UDP-first
selection and fails the retained-TCP control.

Local validation on Go 1.26.3 passed all 25 race verdicts in a verbatim-source
projection, both intended source counterfactuals, and all 64 native runner
contracts (including their actual Go subprocess checks). The first runner
attempt selected the environment's unrelated `go` executable and did not run
four Go checks; the corrected PATH run passed all 64. The projection is not a
complete encrypted native-path pass. Current hosted packet-reference execution
still has to compile and exercise the actual prepared native package.

## docs/testing/native-node-mtu-caller.md

# Native node MTU caller and cleanup ownership

The desktop node-MTU mailbox now has context-aware acquisition, read, mutation,
commit, rollback and close operations. Admission to its owner mutex and private
workspace, immutable command publication, command consumption and the final
commit observation retain the original caller. A cancelled call keeps a pending
command or unconfirmed one-shot commit capability until bounded owned cleanup
drains and restores it. Legacy callers keep their existing method signatures.
Publication admission distinguishes a command that never entered the original
private file creation from a command that may already have been consumed. A
cancelled, unsubmitted begin acquires no lease; an unsubmitted set or commit
retains its prior active lease for rollback. Possible publication still requires
the exact consumed acknowledgement or checked runtime retirement.

`cmd/client/desktop_node_mtu_context_test.go` exercises held owner and mailbox
locks, cancellation while the real private mailbox has an unconsumed mutation,
and cancellation inside the successful final commit observer. Cleanup must use
the exact original lease and, after commit, its original revert capability.
The begin/set/commit publication controls explicitly wait for the held workspace
admission before cancelling, then verify that no request was written and that
cleanup neither strands a nonexistent nonce nor drops the real prior lease.
The private-file child fixture supplies controlled interface readers; these
tests do not claim a physical platform TUN mutation.

Validation on Go 1.26.3, Linux AMD64, November 8, 2026:

- The complete `TestDesktopNodeMTU` selection compiled. The new context tests
  and the existing nonphysical mailbox, replay, replacement, commit, expiry and
  cleanup controls passed.
- Two existing physical-inventory test families stopped during capture with
  `MTU capture has no physical network identity`:
  `TestDesktopNodeMTULateCommitReceiptRestoresExactLiveAndDurableOwner` and
  `TestDesktopNodeMTUCapturedHTTPBridgeCommitsOrRestoresExactNativeReader`.
  Their platform acceptance is unproved in this environment; their guards and
  tests are unchanged.
- The remaining explicit selection passed three consecutive runs:

```sh
go test ./cmd/client -count=3 -run '^TestDesktopNodeMTU(Context|Acknowledgement|CommittedRead|RejectedMutation|ReadBinds|AckWaits|ActualMailbox|FailedBegin|WorkspaceReplacement|ConfirmedCommit|InactiveUnconfirmed|FailedCommitRevert|CloseAccepts|CheckedRuntime|CommitValidation)'
```

This is a prerequisite for the imported-entry MTU adapter. It does not itself
enable imported Auto/Retest or establish physical-device release acceptance.

## docs/testing/native-staging-tor-contextual-admission.md

# Native staging caller errors and Tor contextual admission

## Scope

This repair addresses two failures reported on published commit
`7c4a3eed6be826cfdb846394290a0404daca93a3` by the source test jobs
`128991862808` and `128991862947`. It preserves native staging refusal,
physical-network validation, captured runtime ownership and existing deadlines.

`stageDesktopNodeLaunchContext` already rejected an expired original caller
before creating a private runtime directory. Its early error discarded both the
original `context.Canceled` or `context.DeadlineExceeded` cause and the existing
`errConnectionOperationCancelled` identity. The repair distinguishes a missing
caller from a caller that has expired, and retains both cancellation identities
with the original diagnostic. A live ambient connection context cannot replace
the expired original caller.

The prepared Tor Speed Lab fixture changed the legacy `networkIdentity`
callback. Shipping admission reads its captured `networkIdentityContext`, which
correctly remained unchanged. The fixture now changes that contextual callback.
The production Tor admission check is unchanged: a different, empty, failed or
noncontextual observation cannot authorize a prepared trial or fall back to a
matching legacy observer.

## Executed evidence

The focused tests ran with Go 1.26.3 and the race detector on parent
`1e2ba67ac12bebc10d83fc174db8aee26f267597`. The three relevant production files
were byte-identical to the failing published revision before the staging repair.

The original-source baseline reproduced the existing staging assertion and the
new cancellation and deadline assertions. The missing-caller case and all five
contextual Tor observation cases passed on the original production source.
After the narrow staging correction, all 11 Go test verdicts passed: three root
tests and eight subtests, with no failures or skips. The executed package took
1.084 seconds; compilation is separate from that test duration.

```sh
go test -race -p=1 ./cmd/client \
  -run '^Test(DesktopNativeStaging(OwnsExactDirectoryAndEnvironment|PreservesOriginalCallerRefusal)|SpeedLabTorPreparedAdmissionUsesCapturedContextualNetwork)$' \
  -count=1 -timeout=180s
```

The tests exercise the actual staging and prepared-admission methods. The Tor
unit models only physical observation and uses unstarted command identities; it
is not a native Tor, host routing or device test. It checks that the original
context and deadline reach the captured observer, and that observation does not
change the prior runtime, temporary owner, profile, command or caller lifetime.

## Causal controls

A separate Go overlay restored the original staging error branch and removed
only the prepared-trial contextual network rejection. It changed no working
production files. The cancellation and deadline leaves failed at the original
caller-refusal assertions. All four network refusal leaves failed at the
captured-network admission assertions, while the unchanged contextual observer
and missing-caller cases still passed. The run produced the expected eight
failing verdicts, including the two parent tests, and two passing verdicts, with
no skip, race report or panic. Source hashes were unchanged before and after
all runs.

## Physical observation boundary

The corrected existing process test was attempted once:

```sh
go test -race -p=1 ./cmd/client \
  -run '^TestSpeedLabTorPreparedTrialRejectsStaleAdmissionBeforeStop$/^network$' \
  -count=1 -timeout=180s
```

Its initial public Connect was refused with `operation not permitted` and
`Tor preparation could not capture its physical network`, before the stale-trial
admission boundary. The physical guard was retained, with no local retry or
replacement observer added to that process fixture. Its final hosted result
remains a separate gate. These local results do not establish packaged native,
router, full-suite or release acceptance.

## docs/testing/ordinary-stop-dns.md

# Ordinary Stop and selected-DNS worker retirement

An ordinary desktop Stop now cancels and joins its captured selected-DNS worker
before runtime teardown. The drain precedes the MTU hold because that hold can
itself retire the original native engine to settle an unresolved MTU lease. The
same outer drain covers retained native Stop returns and legacy cleanup.

## Ownership and retry

The Stop receipt retains the exact registered tracker, worker and observation
epoch. The existing 15-second Stop cleanup context bounds cancellation and join;
no application, tracker, native-owner or MTU lock is held while waiting for the
worker's completion receipt. The ordinary operation lock still serializes
teardown. The existing 30-second whole-DNS budget and shorter native operation
deadlines remain unchanged.

Worker admission stays fenced until both DNS cleanup and runtime retirement
succeed. Observations carry an epoch captured with application state. Stop
advances that epoch at retirement admission and again at successful completion,
so a connected snapshot captured before or during Stop cannot start a worker
after a fast reconnect to the same profile and runtime.

A timeout or runtime cleanup error retains the exact fence for an owned Stop
retry. Connect refuses that pending state before preparing a runtime or changing
connection state, including when presentation is already `failed` or `off`.
Session status reports that selected-DNS admission is paused until Stop
completes. A late worker return alone does not clear the fence. A successful Stop
retry reopens the same reusable tracker; terminal controller retirement never
reopens it. Old receipts cannot cancel or clear a replacement tracker, worker or
retirement receipt.

The unified Windows, macOS and Linux primary buttons now expose **Retry
Disconnect** with the exact outstanding receipt, including failed/off
presentation. The [owned retry guide](owned-stop-retry.md) records its dedicated
route, stale-click refusal and separate validation. The macOS Advanced Product
Disconnect, Windows Emergency disconnect and Linux Emergency Stop actions also
remain available. Repeated Connect attempts deliberately keep returning the
pending-cleanup error until an owned Stop retry completes.

## Regression evidence and limits

The original source at `5b76d2fa8a1f09048bfc92890727eb4314aa441f` failed the
actual `stopModeWithIntent` control in 0.01 seconds: Stop returned success while
the entered selected-DNS probe still retained its cleanup. The control holds
cleanup through a channel and checks the original completion receipt; no
observer delay or physical-network failure supplies the expected rejection.

The focused controls exercise actual Stop cancellation, the existing cleanup
deadline, retained failure and explicit retry, same-tracker connection admission
and fresh DNS proof, old observation epochs, exact replacement ownership,
terminal closure, concurrent first status, and contextual connection admission.
The reconnect fixture uses real transaction/session/DNS-worker methods with
modeled runtime publication. It does not establish a native package, physical
device, resolver-network or deployed-router acceptance result.

The focused race run passed 107 test verdicts across 35 roots with no failures
or skips, both before and after rebasing onto
`fb924d4849b448d519c573a78e021b595a216007`. The final execution took 1.582 seconds.
The Stop deadline case uses Go's deterministic test clock; the shipping deadline
remains 15 seconds. Existing backend transaction, kill-switch transition and
seven recovered selected-DNS source-contract controls also passed.

The final publication preserves the concurrent Tor caller change at
`cbc279a744a1ff4a9197951f3ede8708a27a7909`. The Stop production and regression
files are byte-identical to that focused run. Its backend source audit was
aligned with the newly shipping `beginConnectionOperationContext(r.Context())`
call for external Connect; the required original HTTP context replaces the
obsolete context-free marker. Both backend transaction and kill-switch source
audits passed on the combined tree. This rebase is not reported as another
execution of the earlier race matrix.

The existing stale-measurement admission control separately retains the actual
immutable capture and original live caller after joining its periodic observer.
This keeps owner validation independently testable: incidental observer
cancellation cannot make the old operation-reservation order pass.
A temporary source overlay restoring only that old reservation call failed the
updated admission control at its intended assertion in 0.01 seconds, with the
new Stop implementation still present.

The later [public Stop cancellation repair](public-stop-dns-cancellation.md)
adds a captured reservation before public Disconnect and Emergency Stop wait
for `operationMu`. It cancels the original selected-DNS caller at that earlier
boundary, retains the same receipt through admission and join, and refuses a
delayed caller after another owned retry completes. The evidence above remains
the original ordinary-drain component's execution; the later guide records its
separate public-handler, connection-publication and candidate-admission controls.

## docs/testing/original-connection-admission.md

# Original caller during connection and protection admission

## Published behavior

Connection admission can retain an explicit caller through application and
protection locks, private runtime preparation, measurement invalidation and
the final connection transaction. A cancelled caller cannot publish a new
requested connection after waiting for another owner. The admitted operation
keeps that caller's values and deadline. Finishing the original operation does
not cancel or clear a replacement.

Retained protection, pending Off and Linux parent/service observations use the
same caller. Cancellation is an observation failure, never evidence that a
policy is absent. Existing parent and service observation caps remain in force;
an earlier caller deadline also bounds them. No kernel or physical-interface
guard is relaxed.

Recovery and Off admission retain their original caller through the captured
application, protection slot and profile observations. Once Off has completed
native removal and durable publication, joined cleanup keeps that exact result.
Cancellation during later readback cannot pretend that the saved preference
was rolled back or substitute another removal owner.

Compatibility entry points retain their existing behavior. Explicit Tor
public/preflight propagation, fresh Windows native recovery callback contexts
and the final Off rename check are separate follow-ups; this unit does not
claim those additional callers are already converted.

## Executed evidence

The race-instrumented controller image was built with Go 1.26.3 from integrated
snapshot `4fae999264ac44c4d17c2a8ffd7ed250510d3610`. Its exact executable SHA-256
is `1ee10feefad8af34b81e90241297b2f33ee9245f90dd7bfbea7260d2e049c160`. The following focused groups
completed with no failures or skips:

| Selector | Roots | Root and child verdicts |
|---|---:|---:|
| `^TestConnectionAdmissionContext` | 8 | 26 |
| `^TestDesktop(NodeRecoveryContext\|ProtectionOffContext)` | 5 | 25 |
| `TestDesktopNodeJournalOffSavedResultRetryKeepsTokenAndOwner` and `TestDesktopNodeRecoveryOffRetriesActualSavedResult` | 2 | 7 |

Held-lock controls wait until the actual contextual mutex admission observes
the caller, then cancel while the mutex stays held. They do not use a scheduling
delay to infer entry. They retain the original source, slot, controller and
measurement owners and check that an unentered native exchange does not run.
The Linux service tests distinguish a live receipt from one returned after
cancellation. Durable Off retry tests exercise the actual saved file and
retained removal token.

The integration snapshot also contains separately tracked imported Windows
parent changes. This unit's focused admission/recovery tests do not establish
physical Windows WFP behavior, actual Linux parent/service networking, native
packages, publication of those separate features, private deployment or device
acceptance. The follow-up native tests remain required.


---

## docs/testing/owned-stop-retry.md

# Owned pending-Stop retry in the desktop primary action

Windows, macOS and Linux unified shells now show **Retry Disconnect** while an
ordinary Stop still owns unfinished cleanup, including when connection
presentation is already `failed` or `off`. The controller preserves the original
`connected`, phase and runtime fields. It adds `stop_pending` and, only for an
outstanding receipt, its opaque `stop_token` to the authenticated status response.
No error-message parsing supplies this state.

## Captured action and cleanup ownership

Each original Stop receipt owns a fresh random token as well as its registered
tracker, selected-DNS worker and observation epoch. Status captures that receipt
with application presentation in tracker-then-application lock order. It does
not start an observer merely to read status. A missing or replaced owner,
malformed receipt or cancelled status caller produces an error instead of a
false idle view.

The rendered primary button retains its token independently of later polls.
Clicking Retry posts only that token to `POST /api/disconnect/retry`. The dedicated
route acquires the transaction with the original HTTP caller, validates the exact
current receipt before even connection cancellation, and passes the required
receipt into the existing Stop drain. The drain cannot recapture a replacement
receipt. A consumed or replaced token returns a conflict without changing the
new connection. The UI never falls through from a stale Retry to Connect or an
unconditional Disconnect.

The ordinary Disconnect route and its cancellation behavior remain available.
An owned retry uses a non-preempting local request: it cannot cancel another
native UI operation before the controller validates ownership. Windows also
excludes this request from both its queued post-cancel Disconnect and its
completion fallback. An in-progress Stop disables duplicate primary actions.
Existing 15-second Stop cleanup and platform request budgets are unchanged.

The later [public Stop cancellation repair](public-stop-dns-cancellation.md)
keeps the same token-first admission and carries the original Retry caller into
captured cancellation, candidate cleanup and selected-DNS join. Its separate
controls cover cancellation while candidate control is held and while original
entered cleanup remains unjoined. It does not recapture a replacement receipt.

## Persistent mutations and protection recovery

A pending receipt keeps ordinary settings and node mutation controls busy.
The shared controller admission enforces the same rule for ordinary requests
and trusted internal mutations, so an editor opened before Stop cannot write
through `failed` or `off` presentation. New Connect remains fenced by the
published ordinary-Stop guard.

Explicit protection Off retains its existing captured recovery path. Native
UI guards still require a known, disconnected, stable status and the original
captured protection request; enabling protection and ordinary edits cannot use
that exception. The controller checks the parsed policy and refuses every
non-Off preference while Stop is pending. Existing protection ownership,
absence, durable-save and caller checks remain mandatory. Completing Off does
not consume the separate Stop receipt; Retry Disconnect still owns that work.

Windows' Emergency disconnect, Linux's Emergency Stop and the macOS advanced
Disconnect action remain available. The primary Retry action now gives the
pending receipt a direct visible recovery path.

## Executed evidence

The controller run was based on published
`ef8d3762ee2e9e5f8f742c66ea3a8f44baa9ba8b` plus this change. Go 1.26.3 with the
race detector passed **46 verdicts across 19 roots**, with zero failures or
skips, in **1.448 seconds** of test execution. It includes:

- Original ordinary Stop failure, token-bound cleanup, same-tracker reconnect
  and a subsequent stale retry that cannot affect that reconnect.
- Consumed receipts, replacement trackers with the same epoch, changed tokens,
  controller closure, queued receipt replacement and original caller expiry.
- Status cancellation behind tracker/application locks, unchanged Connected
  presentation, invalid owners and no observer creation for a status-only read.
- Required-receipt drain refusal, strict request decoding, ordinary/internal
  mutation rejection in both failed/off phases, non-Off protection refusal and
  successful owned Off with the Stop fence retained.
- The existing ordinary Stop join, deadline, observation-epoch, replacement,
  terminal-retirement and independent stale-measurement admission controls.

Before publication the unit was rebased onto
`fcc6f47a1d73d6665aff505333e5fb2f8ec7cac6`, preserving its four unrelated MAX
runner/generator repairs. Every tested production, test and workflow file was
byte-identical after that rebase. This comparison is not another execution of
the earlier race or host-callback gates.

Three bridge tests also passed with the race detector, in 1.035 seconds. The
actual framed bridge preserved the retry route, token bytes and HTTP conflict.
The controller bearer-owner test includes the new route, and the executable
Windows managed target validator accepts its canonical local path while
refusing foreign authorities and path aliases. Authentication and native IPC
allowlists were not widened.

The actual transformed Windows PowerShell, macOS Swift and Linux C primary
callbacks were executed with modeled window/dispatch/HTTP boundaries. The
Windows test used PowerShell 7.6.9, the Swift test used Swift 6.4.2, and the C test
used real json-glib. Their positive cases cover rendered and freshly observed
retry tokens, replacement status, server refusal, malformed receipts, ordinary
Connect/Disconnect, local operation ownership, ordinary mutation blocking and
explicit Off recovery. Eleven deliberate source mutations were rejected: five
Windows controls, three Swift controls and three C controls. The Linux fixture
also executes the transformed refresh prefix that paints and binds the button.
Existing protection and compact-DNS ownership/serialization fixtures passed on
all three host runtimes. The Windows/Linux shipping mutation audits, macOS
shipping Swift parse/audit, backend transaction audit, kill-switch contract and
native workflow parity audit also passed.

The new host callback gate is mandatory in the focused native workflows and
matching Windows/macOS/Linux release jobs. Windows jobs require both
PowerShell 7 and Windows PowerShell 5.1; Linux release jobs cover both existing
architectures. Source-audit controls reject removal or optional execution of
these steps. These workflow requirements are not reported as already completed
native package executions.

The local callback fixtures do not establish a WPF/AppKit/GTK application run,
real firewall removal, physical-device networking, a complete native package,
current release acceptance or deployment. The successful protection Off test
uses the existing modeled kernel owner while executing real controller methods.
The separate earlier wait before a public Disconnect acquires `operationMu`
still needs its captured-owner cancellation hook; this retry UX does not claim
to close that boundary.

## docs/testing/private-throughput-clock.md

# Checked private-node throughput intervals

The direct private-node throughput implementation in `telemetry_hops.go` now
uses a separate checked platform interval for each HTTP download and upload.
The existing `telemetry.go` connection-speed endpoint and per-node routed-speed
endpoints share this implementation. Public signatures, request timeout,
selected API/token, exact requested byte count, admission and captured path
observers remain unchanged.

Each clock starts immediately before its original HTTP request. Download timing
ends only after the exact bounded body is read and closed. Upload timing ends
after the complete bounded acknowledgement body is read and closed, preserving
acknowledged-byte validation. Random payload preparation remains outside the
upload interval. Mbps uses the actual byte count and the unrounded checked
milliseconds; only the resulting display value is rounded. Millisecond output
retains real tiny positive measurements instead of truncating them to zero.

Initialization/read errors, cancellation, nonpositive/nonfinite durations and
nonfinite or nonpositive rounded rates return an empty result. Download timing
failure prevents the upload. Upload failure cannot return a successful partial
download score. Original clock and cancellation causes remain discoverable.
There is no fallback clock, minimum duration, retry or substitute measurement.
Wall-clock timestamps still identify when the result was produced.

## Executable evidence

`throughput_clock_test.go` exercises the shipping transfer implementation against
actual ephemeral HTTP listeners with only the per-call interval factory modeled.
Its matrix covers both directions, one-nanosecond/fractional/ordinary intervals,
clock initialization/read failures, invalid timing, rate overflow and caller
cancellation during readback. It verifies exact transferred bytes, original
credentials, encoding, selected node identity and proof label, server timing,
no network on initial clock failure and no upload after failed download timing.

Additional tests reject short/excess download bodies, rejected HTTP status,
redirects, malformed/wrong/oversized acknowledgements, and verify a real platform
clock through the unchanged production entry point. A server holds the upload
acknowledgement after flushing its first bytes: interval readback must wait for
the complete body. This distinguishes full acknowledged transfer timing from
request transmission or response-header timing.

The final selected Linux race run passed **70 verdicts across 13 roots**. A
separate existing owner regression run passed **16 verdicts across 5 roots**,
selecting the single-hop and connection cases to avoid overlapping reserved
proxy ports used by other work. These cover active node/session/profile
replacement, request cancellation, unproved preflight, successful transfers,
shared admission, captured MTU retirement and keeping the original VPN running.
No existing negative cases were removed or weakened.

Commands (Go 1.26.3, workspace `GOCACHE` and `GOTMPDIR`):

```sh
go test -race ./cmd/client -run '^Test(PrivateThroughput|PrivateTelemetryBoundary|PrivateBenchmarkRequestContext|RoutedSpeedRejectsProfileReplacedBeforeObserverStarts|ProfileSpeedTestRejectsNonActiveSingleHopNode|StandaloneThroughputWaits)' -count=1
go test -race ./cmd/client -run '^Test(RoutedSpeedRequests|RoutedSpeedCancelsWhenEitherHopProfileChanges|StandaloneThroughputSharesAdmission)/(single|connection)$' -count=1
```

A source overlay deliberately reinstated a `.001 ms` floor independently in
both transfer directions. The actual HTTP tests failed for both tiny samples:
`0.000001 ms` became `0.001 ms`, changing the corresponding measured Mbps by a
factor of 1,000. The overlay was external to the repository; the shipping files
remain the passing source. This demonstrates sensitivity to fabricated timing
and rates, rather than only the existence of a clock helper.

## Scope

This unit covers direct private-node transfers only. The separate reserved proxy
lane and Speed Lab transfer loops still require their own checked interval
adoption. It does not claim native Windows throughput runtime evidence or alter
Compare ranking, packet protection, path restoration, or endpoint ownership.


---

## docs/testing/proxy-throughput-clock.md

# Checked throughput on the original reserved hop lane

`measureRoutedProfileSpeedViaProxyContext` now starts a checked platform interval
for each real download and upload, through a private per-call factory. The
original public signatures and reserved proxy client remain unchanged. This
adopts the published direct-transfer duration/rate validation for the separate
proxy-lane implementation without merging the two transport paths.

The same paired node is still proved before any transfer and again after the
complete successful upload. Clock initialization follows the initial proof;
failure sends no benchmark request for that direction. A download clock failure
prevents upload, and upload failure cannot salvage a download-only result.
Both proofs still use the captured private API/token and original reserved lane.
Cancellation after the final proof is checked before returning the measurement.

Each elapsed interval includes the original request and complete bounded body;
upload still requires its exact byte acknowledgement. Random payload generation
is outside the upload interval. Unrounded measured elapsed determines Mbps;
real positive tiny durations survive millisecond formatting. Invalid timing or
rates return an empty result, preserving original error/cancellation causes.
No fallback timer, duration floor or proof retry is introduced.

## Verification

The new actual reserved-lane HTTP matrix exercises both direction clocks and
checks both successful identity proofs, wrong initial/final identities, wrong
acknowledgements, clock initialization/read errors, invalid timing, rate
overflow, cancellation, exact bytes, endpoint/token identity and tiny/ordinary
measurements. Existing lane, upload boundary, routed ownership and published
RTT tests are included in the selected race run.

The selected Linux race run passed **118 verdicts across 17 roots**, including
the full existing routed/standalone owner cases on both reserved lanes and the
published Speed Lab/hop-lane RTT clock tests. No existing negative cases were
changed. Command:

```sh
go test -race ./cmd/client -run '^Test(ProxyThroughput|MultihopLane|MultihopUploadAcknowledgement|SpeedLabAndHopLaneUseCheckedIntervals|SpeedLabLatencyStatisticsRetainMeasuredTinyValues|SpeedLabProbeProductionClockAndCancelledCaller|RoutedSpeedRequests|RoutedSpeedCancelsWhenEitherHopProfileChanges|StandaloneThroughput)' -count=1
```

A source overlay replaced proxy throughput interval readback with an invented
`1 ms` success. All four actual HTTP read/cancel-read cases failed as expected: the
mutated source returned a completed throughput result despite the original
clock failure. The overlay lived outside the repository; the shipping source
retains the checked readback and its passing results.

## Limits

These are Linux race tests with real local HTTP exchanges and explicitly modeled
paired node responses. They do not establish native Windows device throughput
results. Speed Lab's independent transfer and whole-load clocks remain a
separate source unit. The captured graph/MTU/admission owners and cleanup rules
are unchanged.
