## docs/testing/public-stop-dns-cancellation.md

# Public Stop cancellation before transaction admission

Desktop `POST /api/disconnect` and `POST /api/emergency-stop` now cancel their
captured selected-DNS worker before waiting for the connection transaction.
This closes the earlier admission gap left by the
[ordinary Stop drain](ordinary-stop-dns.md): a DNS result can own `operationMu`
while entering an existing native protection guard, so waiting for that lock
before cancelling its caller could leave Disconnect waiting for the DNS budget.

## Captured reservation and caller

Public Stop reserves the exact registered tracker, selected-DNS worker,
observation epoch and random retry token. Tracker and application state are
captured in that order. It cancels the original DNS context and the captured
connection, startup and recovery contexts, and cancels the candidate owner
under its original control. These are nonjoining context cancellations. The
application lock is released before acquiring candidate control; no native or
DNS join takes place under the tracker, application or candidate-control locks.

The reservation remains visible through the existing `stop_pending` and
`stop_token` status fields. Public operation-lock admission uses the original
HTTP caller. After admission it requires the same receipt pointer, registered
tracker, worker, epoch and token. It cannot recapture an owner after another
Stop or owned Retry has completed the original request. A late caller returns a
conflict and cannot cancel or disconnect a replacement connection.

A caller cancelled before capture creates no Stop receipt and changes no
session history. Emergency Stop records its reason only after successful owned
capture. Expiry after capture retains the receipt for explicit Retry Disconnect.
The selected-DNS drain uses the original HTTP caller within the existing
15-second Stop cleanup ceiling. The DNS worker's 30-second whole-operation
budget and shorter native operation deadlines are unchanged.

`POST /api/disconnect/retry` still validates its displayed token under
`operationMu` before cancellation. It now carries the same caller and required
receipt through cancellation and DNS cleanup. Public candidate cancellation
and cleanup also retain that caller, with the existing 10-second candidate
cleanup ceiling. Entered candidate cleanup remains joined; cancellation does
not authorize detaching its original owner.

These caller-aware waits do not change every legacy runtime teardown helper.
Existing subprocess, protection, MTU and native retirement semantics remain
in place; this change does not claim a new end-to-end deadline for all possible
platform cleanup operations.

## Admission while Stop is pending

A transition which already owned `operationMu` may retire its original runtime,
but its internal Stop cannot consume a public reservation. The reservation is
cleared only after successful cleanup using that exact required receipt, or by
terminal shutdown after the tracker has permanently closed. A timeout or native
cleanup error preserves the original retry state. Successful owned Retry permits
a new connection and a fresh selected-DNS proof on the same reusable tracker.

Connection context publication now rechecks the pending receipt atomically
with application state, after the existing filesystem and protection preflight.
This covers a Stop arriving after the early admission check but before the new
connection context exists. A context published first is cancelled as the
original owner; a Stop reserved first prevents later publication.

Candidate benchmarks have a separate operation-owner publication point. That
publication now holds tracker, candidate-control and application locks in that
order and rejects a pending Stop, including `failed` or `off` presentation. When
no tracker exists, it holds candidate control and application state, rechecks
registration, and retries if a tracker appeared. It creates no observer merely
to start a benchmark. An already admitted candidate remains the exact owner
cancelled and joined by public Stop.

Terminal controller shutdown keeps its existing permanent admission barrier,
observer cancellation and selected-DNS retirement before operation-lock
admission. It does not reopen the reusable public fence or adopt a replacement
tracker.

## Executable evidence and limits

On original source `e2b21c875195df3a75065f27230ffa1774d977a3`, the actual
registered DNS worker reached its normal terminal adoption and owned
`operationMu` while waiting for the protection mutex. Both actual public routes
then failed the cancellation-order control in **0.055 seconds**: the original
DNS caller remained live at the existing connection-cancellation boundary.
The test releases its held mutex and joins its worker without advancing the
30-second DNS deadline. An earlier fixture used `synctest.Wait` with a plain
mutex waiter; its 60-second timeout is retained as fixture diagnosis and is not
counted as the causal rejection.

The focused execution covers both public routes, original caller cancellation
before capture and during lock/join waits, exact registry/epoch/worker
replacement, repeated Stop, internal cleanup while a public reservation is
pending, delayed public calls after owned Retry and reconnect, atomic actual
Connect publication, actual candidate handler admission, and terminal closure.
Existing ordinary Stop, owned Retry, measurement admission, recovery and
candidate lifecycle controls remain included. Source contracts retain the
original DNS-before-MTU/native teardown order and reject removal of caller or
required-receipt propagation in the public handlers.

The final Go 1.26.3 race run passed **151 verdicts across 62 roots**, with no
failures or skips, in **20.477 seconds**. It ran on published parent
`f23dea2f6d1650bac010cd569ee27392651b539d` plus this frozen unit. The earlier
146-verdict run is retained as intermediate evidence; the final run also
includes the exact Retry cancellation and candidate cleanup caller controls.
The backend transaction audit, owned retry route/removal contracts and
kill-switch transition contract passed with the updated call chain.

One isolated overlay image exercised three disjoint causal controls against
the same final tests. Restoring the earlier connection publication code made
the actual transaction publish a new context after Stop captured cancellation.
Removing public-reservation retention let an internal transition consume the
waiting Stop receipt. Removing the candidate pending check let the actual
handler publish a measured receipt in both failed/off cases. All three roots
failed at those intended assertions in **2.549 seconds**, with seven failing
root/subtest verdicts and no panic or data-race report. Each root reaches only
its corresponding altered boundary. The overlay did not modify shipping
files; all frozen source and contract hashes were verified afterward.

The tests execute the shipping controller and DNS/candidate ownership methods.
Resolver responses, candidate lanes and platform protection helpers are
explicitly modeled where needed. They do not establish device networking,
firewall removal, a complete native package, a deployed router or release
acceptance. Physical-network guards and native acceptance gates are unchanged.

## docs/testing/routechoice-identity-caller.md

# Comparison identity keeps the probe caller

Local/Server comparison proves both routed operands and pins one session
identity across them. Native identity observation may acquire retained locks or
perform IPC. Giving those observations an unrelated background context would
let cancelled or expired measurement work wait beyond its original authority.

The optional `routechoice.ContextIdentitySession` capability supplies
`IdentityContext(context.Context) (string, error)`. The comparison engine uses
it for initial identity capture and every post-proof/probe identity check. The
initial capture and path proof share the same original proof deadline; each
post-probe identity uses that probe's exact context before it is cancelled.
An identity error, empty identity, replacement or value returned after
cancellation disqualifies the candidate without a score or legacy fallback.
The lowest valid mean, one shared random external endpoint, repeated final
probes and owned teardown remain unchanged.

Existing synchronous `Session.Identity` implementations remain compatible and
joined, with caller checks before and after. This shared contract does not make
those legacy callbacks interruptible. Shipping native hosts must explicitly
implement the context capability.

All nineteen shared route-choice test roots pass with race instrumentation
(45 verdicts). New controls retain original context values and exact per-phase
deadlines, reject identity failures/cancellation at all four observation
boundaries, and revoke a winning candidate whose identity changes after its
final external probe. The surviving candidate must reprove both operands
through the same captured target; the prior candidate is closed first.

## Desktop native host wiring

The desktop Tor comparison session and the desktop multihop execution session
now implement the contextual capability. The Tor driver carries the caller
through its captured application, selected profile, entry source, physical
network, temporary Speed Lab owner and native comparison identity. A changed
or missing observer cannot fall back to an ambient observation. Probe and
retained-path checks use the same context, including after native measurement
workers drain.

The combined desktop controller compiled with Go 1.26.3 race instrumentation.
Sixteen focused Tor comparison roots passed all 65 root/subtest verdicts. This
includes four new host identity roots and the existing native mailbox,
original-caller, cancellation, replacement, retryable-close and actual-process
join controls. The tests retain actual private IPC files and child processes;
their timing receipts are fixtures, not evidence of a Tor network circuit or
physical-device acceptance.

This identity change bounds comparison identity and probe admission. The
durable-choice follow-up below closes a separate caller boundary; public
connection capture, preflight and prelaunch propagation remain in progress.

## Durable choice and final adoption

Saving a proved Tor choice now retains both its original connection operation
and the original request/adoption lifetime. The resulting caller preserves the
request's values and the earlier deadline, and either original can cancel it.
Native proof, source and physical-network observations, application/native
locks, profile persistence and Startup Last persistence use that same caller.
Recorded Disconnect intent rejects admission even before the original cancel
callback has returned. Cleanup keeps its separately owned authority.

The final atomic rename rechecks the caller and exact current owner. A rejected
write preserves the original file and rolls back only this operation's in-memory
choice. A completed durable profile write remains recorded if later adoption
fails; it is not falsely reported as rolled back. Connected still requires the
fresh retained proofs and the original current path after persistence.

The integrated race binary at
`0de31ff7103622fa999da523061581623dece47c` passed all 23 verdicts across five new
runtime roots. They exercise held application/proof/native-owner locks,
independently cancelled original lifetimes, the actual Disconnect function with
its cancel callback held, actual private atomic writes and a retained private
IPC proof process. The final-rename cases preserve the old inode and contents
when admission fails. The controller AST contract passed ten verdicts; all 43
Python Tor source-audit tests also passed, including removed-guard controls.

The broader selector additionally reached twelve older physical-capture cases,
which failed before their assertions because this environment cannot capture
the physical network. Those twelve leaves and their parent are retained as
failed results, not passed or skipped cases. The local controlled-observer
checks do not replace the hosted native gate, a real Tor circuit, or device
acceptance. Public connection preflight remains a separate unfinished unit.

## docs/testing/routed-measurement-clock.md

# Checked clocks for routed candidates and Compare responses

This source unit changes the two routed RTT samplers in
`via_entry_candidates.go` and `multihop_execution.go`. It leaves request
lifetimes, receipt expiry, pacing, startup and cleanup clocks unchanged.

The candidate sampler still opens TCP through the exact captured entry lane.
Discovery remains separate from the requested samples. Each measured handshake
now owns a checked `hopmeasure` interval through the existing client clock
helper. An initialization or read failure, or a nonpositive/nonfinite interval,
ends that candidate without a score. Clock failure during port discovery does
not try another port; failure after successful samples cannot salvage a partial
score. The returned socket closes before timing failure returns. A nil socket
cannot establish a successful handshake. Real network failures retain their
original failed-sample count and disqualification behavior.

The previous `math.Max(.001, elapsed)` fabricated a minimum routed RTT. It is
removed. A real tiny positive sample keeps the already published client
summary precision behavior and can be ranked without being replaced by an
invented minimum. Existing candidate ranking still uses the 10% trimmed mean,
requires all 50 samples, and rejects failed or out-of-bounds results. Both entry
proofs, private lane ownership, candidate cleanup, stale receipt rejection and
unchanged Home/profile state retain their existing code.

The Compare response sampler uses `hopmeasure.Interval.Duration` directly,
retaining complete nanoseconds without a millisecond round trip. Clock failures
preserve their cause, and zero or negative durations are rejected at the sampler.
After readback, the original session/command/graph identity and paired node proof
still validate before a duration is returned. Cancellation cannot adopt a late
read. Both last-node and external-response methods use this shared sampler.

The exact Compare formula, one randomly selected endpoint for the entire
comparison, both winner re-probes, teardown, fallback and restoration logic are
unchanged. They remain owned by the existing routechoice and execution code.

## Verification

The focused new client race tests passed on Go 1.26.3/Linux. Candidate tests
exercise the actual sample loop through an explicitly modeled captured lane
with owned connections, checking 50 samples plus discovery, tiny and ordinary
values, initialization/read failures, invalid values, late failure, cancellation,
retired lane, missing socket and a real failed-sample result. Compare tests use
the actual reserved HTTP proof proxy and original session checks; only the
physical inventory and retained process handle are modeled. They verify exact
nanosecond/fractional durations, no HTTP on clock initialization failure, no
retries, original error cause, cancellation, replaced command, changed graph
and wrong paired node proof.

The complete `internal/routechoice` race suite passed **45 verdicts across 19
roots**, including exact score arithmetic, target capture, both probes and final
rechecks, cleanup failure, original caller/deadline and score clearing. The
broader selected run produced **155 passing client verdicts across 53 roots**
and **42 passing hopmeasure verdicts across 16 roots**.

That broader run was not an all-pass result: its unrelated nested
`TestDesktopMultihopCandidateContextOwnsPreflight` recompilation was killed
(`compile: signal: killed`) before assertions. The existing pinned native CLI
case skipped because no exact native executable was supplied. Neither test was
changed or relabeled as passing. Full matching-host native execution remains
required. A later control attempt initially encountered a full `/home/agent`
tmpfs; rerunning uses a copied build cache on the workspace filesystem and a
bounded compiler memory setting.

## Causal controls and limits

Two disposable source-overlay controls retain the same executable tests:

- Reinstating the candidate's 0.001 ms floor failed the tiny-value case after
  all 51 original connections had opened and closed. The returned 50-sample
  distribution showed 0.001 instead of the original 0.000001 ms values.
- Replacing Compare duration readback with a fabricated nanosecond failed nine
  sample cases, including fractional precision, read errors, zero/negative
  readings, cancellation and replaced owners. Read-count assertions also reject
  substituting a plausible duration without reading the retained clock.

The overlays did not modify repository files. Both controls completed after
moving their build-cache writes off the exhausted home tmpfs.

These are source/component results. They do not prove current-SHA packages,
physical routing, actual Windows performance-counter execution, deployment,
or remaining throughput-clock adoption. No Compare capability exclusion or
original product requirement is removed by this change.

## docs/testing/session-dns-lifecycle.md

# Selected DNS worker retirement

The session tracker now retains each asynchronous selected-DNS proof from
admission through completion. Its original 30-second worker budget reaches
tracker, app and path admission as well as resolver work. A session, node,
runtime, base or profile change cancels a worker even before the detailed path
observer has been installed. The next proof cannot replace an unjoined worker.

Controller shutdown closes DNS-proof admission and cancels the captured worker
before waiting for the connection transaction. It joins that same worker under
a five-second cleanup wait and retains it for retry if cleanup is incomplete.
The native connection fixture also retires and joins its own captured tracker.
An old tracker cannot retire or publish into a replacement tracker with the
same visible session ID.

This addresses the asynchronous DNS setup remaining in the synctest bubble
after `TestDesktopNodePublicSpeedLabCancellationRestoresExactWinner` exited in
the source job for commit `2926063d498d7e4a5889fc3e73f3fc399fb536c5`
(Actions run `42413089753`, job `128525252342`). That observation establishes a
missing fixture lifetime/join; it does not establish a permanent device deadlock.

## Focused verification

Go 1.26.3, Linux AMD64, November 8, 2026 (America/Chicago):

```sh
go test -race -json ./cmd/client \
  -run '^(TestSessionDNSWorker|TestDNSProofPath|TestControllerShutdown(CancelsBeforeWaiting|RejectsForeign|RetainsTerminal|BindFailure))' \
  -count=1 -timeout=90s
```

All 52 test verdicts passed. The new tests exercise held app admission,
cancelled entered resolver work, retained cleanup and retry, controller drain,
same-ID tracker replacement, and stale observation before path admission.
The existing DNS path tests retain graph, credential, policy and publication
rejection coverage.

Four source mutations were separately rejected by the intended assertions:

| Mutation | Observed rejection |
| --- | --- |
| Replace the original admission context with Background | Cancelled setup cannot join while the original app lock is held |
| Remove worker cancellation from terminal retirement | The original admission outlives the cleanup deadline |
| Return without joining an entered probe | Retirement reports success while its resolver remains entered |
| Remove cancellation at stale observation | Seven changed-owner cases remain live; the unchanged capture still passes |

The full public Speed Lab fixture and an existing Speed Lab shutdown fixture
cannot reach their assertions in this local workspace: physical interface
capture returns `operation not permitted`. They remain enabled for native CI.
This focused verification does not substitute for native networking, leak,
device, or exact-release acceptance.

## docs/testing/speed-lab-hop-latency-clock.md

# Checked Speed Lab and per-hop HTTP latency

The fixed one-byte Speed Lab probe and the private paired-node hop-lane
sampler now capture a checked platform interval for each actual HTTP exchange.
The original HTTP client, resolver/path capture, deadlines, byte limits,
credentials, identity proof and cancellation remain in place. These are HTTP
response measurements, not ICMP pings.

A failed clock initialization sends no request. Failed readback, nonpositive or
nonfinite timing, and cancellation cannot produce a successful sample. The hop
sampler discards the whole observation if a later clock or paired-node proof
fails; earlier samples cannot be reused as proof of that failed observation.
Speed Lab retains its existing failed-probe accounting and requires the same
minimum number of valid idle and loaded samples.

The latency summaries retain real positive tiny values when ordinary display
rounding would erase them. Ordinary values keep the existing three-decimal
precision. The stable incremental mean preserves a constant tiny sample, and
zero jitter remains valid for equal samples. Speed Lab statistics reject
nonpositive/nonfinite input rather than emitting invalid JSON values or a
fabricated successful latency.

## Observed checks

Go 1.26.3/Linux completed the new clock suite with **23 passing race verdicts
across three roots** in 1.466 seconds. The tests use an actual isolated TLS peer
for the fixed Speed Lab request and the actual reserved HTTP proxy for the
paired-node lane. Clock factories are private per-call seams; production has no
mutable global clock or request override.

The cases cover initialization/read errors, true tiny and ordinary timings,
zero/negative/nonfinite readings, cancellation during readback, wrong response
length, a late wrong node identity, and late failure after a good hop sample.
They check the original private token/URL, one-byte provider contract, sample
counts, error cause, no network work after initialization failure, and the
production interval on a real TLS exchange.

Existing cancellation, direction deadline, partial/acknowledged upload,
captured-tunnel, redirect, provider-failure and lane-ownership regressions
completed with **49 passing race verdicts across 26 roots** in 6.814 seconds:

```sh
go test -race -count=1 ./cmd/client \
  -run '^Test(SpeedLab(Direction|Transfer|RoundCalibration|CapturedTunnel|MeasurementConstructors|Duration|Pattern|Stable)|MultihopLane|ConnectedMultihopLatency|MultihopLiveLatency)' \
  -timeout=150s
```

A disposable source overlay quantized each actual successful sample before
publication. Both exact tiny-value tests failed: the TLS probe returned zero
after one exchange and the paired lane returned zero after two authenticated
exchanges. The control completed with test failures, not a compile failure.
The overlay did not alter repository source. Whitespace validation passed.

This unit concerns latency. Throughput transfer/round/whole-load timing has
separate code and must preserve actual transferred bytes and acknowledged
uploads when adopting checked clocks. Current-SHA native packages, physical
path/network-transition acceptance and private deployment remain distinct.


---

## docs/testing/speed-lab-throughput-clock.md

# Checked elapsed time for Speed Lab throughput

Speed Lab now measures each single transfer, joined parallel round and complete
load window with a checked platform duration. Its private per-call clock factory
preserves nanosecond durations without a floating-point round trip. Public
transfer and measurement signatures remain unchanged, including the callers
that measure the original imported entry lane.

Scheduling still uses the existing monotonic timeout/pacing clocks. Each
transfer interval starts immediately before HTTP work and ends at the original
body-read boundary, before deferred response cleanup. Parallel timing brackets
all streams and their joined completion. The whole-load interval begins at the
load window and ends after its final round, before loaded-sampler cancellation
and join overhead. A stalled final tail therefore remains in the throughput
denominator. Pattern generation and setup remain outside the load window.

## Bytes, acknowledgement and cancellation

The existing exact download limits, consumed upload-body counts, HTTP status and
bounded complete upload response checks remain intact. Failed upload streams
still contribute zero bytes to a parallel round. An ordinary administrative
direction deadline can retain bytes actually received in a partial download or
only the fully acknowledged upload streams. An outer request cancellation
always rejects the result.

A distinct clock-failure sentinel preserves the original clock cause and cannot
be classified as an ordinary deadline, including when joined with cancellation
or `DeadlineExceeded` from the same request. Failed clock initialization sends
no work for that interval. Nonpositive durations or failed readback cannot
salvage earlier completed rounds or acknowledged upload bytes into a successful
measurement. Parallel aggregation retains a clock failure even when another
stream first failed for a different reason.

Rates use actual byte counts and measured durations. Tiny positive whole-load
seconds and imported-entry millisecond output survive display rounding rather
than becoming zero. Ordinary display precision, jitter and latency behavior are
unchanged; no duration floor or fallback clock is introduced.

## Test reference clocks

Two existing upload fixtures compared the production outer duration with a
transport/acknowledgement duration measured by `time.Since`. On Windows, a coarse
timing tick can overstate that inner interval relative to the actual QPC
interval, making the ordering assertion invalid. Both fixture reference
intervals now use checked Duration readback. Their cancellable ticker waits,
outer-versus-inner ordering assertions and byte/acknowledgement/cancellation
checks are retained without wider tolerances or waits.

## Executable evidence

The new HTTP tests exercise successful one-nanosecond and ordinary intervals,
initialization/read errors and nonpositive durations through both single and
parallel download/upload operations. Actual transferred bytes, provider,
headers, independently owned interval counts and no work after initialization
failure are checked.

Direction tests send a verified partial download and stall the final body until
the real administrative deadline. A separately controlled whole-load interval
must determine the reported denominator. Failures at transfer, parallel or
whole-load readback must return no result. Upload tests first complete and
acknowledge one payload, then stall the second acknowledgement: a clock failure
at any level must invalidate the entire result, preserving the earlier byte
accounting without publishing it as successful throughput.

The final focused race run passed **101 verdicts across 18 roots**. It includes
the published RTT clock tests, loaded-sampler stop semantics, existing provider
failure/cancellation/partial-byte tests and original imported-entry proof and
transfer-count tests. A further captured-tunnel regression run passed **16
verdicts across 6 roots**, including actual public TLS transfer, foreign TLS and
redirect rejection, late response/dial cancellation and separate admission and
socket lifetime. All five code/test files exactly matched the isolated copies.

Commands (Go 1.26.3, workspace build cache and temporary directory):

```sh
go test -race ./cmd/client -run '^TestSpeedLab(TransferCheckedClock|DirectionCheckedClock|UploadDeadlineCannotHideClockFailure|TransferUploadRequiresConsumedRequestBytes|TransferDoesNotInheritEnvironmentProxy|TransferParallelRejectsPartialUploads|Direction|RoundCalibration|ImportedHopUsesOriginalEntryLaneAndActualTransferCounts|AndHopLaneUseCheckedIntervals|LatencyStatisticsRetainMeasuredTinyValues|ProbeProductionClockAndCancelledCaller|LoadedSamplerDoesNotCountIntentionalStopAsFailure)' -count=1
go test -race ./cmd/client -run '^TestSpeedLab(CapturedTunnel|MeasurementConstructors)' -count=1
```

Two source overlays completed with the expected assertion failures:

- Removing clock-failure precedence from context-error classification made both
  partial-download cases and both acknowledged-upload cases incorrectly publish
  successful results. All four cases failed: the mutated download retained 256
  bytes, and the mutated upload published its earlier 65,536 acknowledged bytes
  despite a clock failure on the final tail.
- Replacing only the whole-load checked interval with `time.Since` failed the
  controlled denominator case: it reported `0.351 s` instead of
  the supplied `2 s` interval and never read the original whole-load clock.

Both overlays were external to the repository. The shipping source remains the
passing checked implementation. Validation uses the isolated `a0fbb61d` checkout plus precisely this
unit because unrelated Linux platform capture files were concurrently under
construction in the shared checkout. Initial shared-tree attempts stopped at
those unrelated compilation errors before executing tests.

## Scope

This is source and Linux local HTTP/race evidence, not a claim of native Windows
or mobile device throughput. Captured path ownership, endpoint identity,
Compare ranking, imported transport policy and restoration remain with their
existing callers. Matching-host runtime validation remains separate.


---

## docs/testing/tor-original-callers.md

# Tor preparation retains its original callers

This follow-up completes the public preparation and prelaunch caller boundaries
left open in [the comparison identity guide](routechoice-identity-caller.md).
It preserves the selected graph and the existing proof, cleanup and adoption
requirements. It does not establish physical Tor or device acceptance.

## Shipping behavior

- **Original request and operation.** Public Connect, external-entry Connect,
  Compare and temporary Speed Lab preparation retain the original request or
  intent together with the captured connection operation. Initial registry
  locks, selection and predecessor capture, resolution, runtime preparation,
  preflight and prelaunch observe that caller. Either original lifetime can
  cancel work. A recorded Disconnect intent rejects adoption even while its
  cancellation callback is held. See
  [the captured caller](../../cmd/client/desktop_tor_caller.go) and
  [its causal controls](../../cmd/client/desktop_tor_caller_context_test.go).
- **Independent restoration.** The Speed Lab coordinator passes its existing
  `restoreCtx` through restart to the first restore-entry lock. Restoration
  remains independent of the cancelled temporary-test HTTP request, while its
  own expiry or Disconnect still stops admission. This preserves the existing
  separately owned cleanup allowance; it does not create a replacement budget.
- **Verification and private inputs.** Shipping runtime preparation uses the
  contextual runtime callback. Native and Tor version subprocesses inherit
  the original caller and retain their existing 10-second and 5-second limits.
  Cancellation joins the actual verification child. Private input capture,
  hashing and staging remain synchronous and retain caller checks and file
  identity protections. The capture reader returns literal `io.EOF` for a
  healthy completed read, while cancellation at read completion retains its
  context cause. This avoids treating successful hashing as an EOF failure.
  See [runtime preparation](../../cmd/client/desktop_tor_prepare.go),
  [Tor verification](../../cmd/client/bundled_tor.go) and
  [reader controls](../../cmd/client/desktop_tor_capture_reader_test.go).
- **Endpoint observations.** The shipping resolver uses the real
  `physicalNetworkIdentityContext` and `net.DefaultResolver.LookupNetIP`.
  The first network observation uses the original caller. Resolution and the
  final observation share the same remaining 10-second lookup context.
  Cancellation, expiry, changed networks and late results reject the lookup
  without replacing its original failure cause. The
  [endpoint controls](../../cmd/client/desktop_tor_endpoint_context_test.go)
  exercise each observation boundary.
- **Retained live monitor.** Periodic health checks use the captured contextual
  network observer under the existing live-monitor authority. Completing the
  HTTP request does not retire a valid connection. Monitor cancellation and
  joined retirement retain their existing implementation; this unit verifies
  observer selection and continuing ownership after HTTP completion. The
  [monitor controls](../../cmd/client/desktop_tor_monitor_context_test.go)
  witness a real fixture child through a completed monitor cycle and reject
  failed or foreign observations.
- **Initial prelaunch expiry.** A missing caller is rejected separately from
  an expired original caller. Cancellation or deadline expiry preserves
  `errTorPrelaunchRejected`, `errConnectionOperationCancelled` and the actual
  context cause. No admission callback or child starts, and the exact prepared
  workspace and DNS/native ownership are cleaned through the existing owner.
  [Prepared-owner controls](../../cmd/client/desktop_tor_prelaunch_cause_test.go)
  cover cancelled, expired and missing callers, including idempotent cleanup.

## Recorded execution

The following focused groups ran in the Go 1.26.3 race-instrumented controller
at local integrated snapshot
`7060aae83d6d15d135d6f510af5447ba7b7b4e51`. A verdict here includes a root or a
subtest; these counts are not distinct product requirements. Every listed
group had zero failures and zero skips.

| Evidence log | Test roots | Passing verdicts |
|---|---:|---:|
| `tor-caller.jsonl` | 9 | 32 |
| `tor-endpoint.jsonl` | 4 | 18 |
| `tor-monitor.jsonl` | 2 | 9 |
| `tor-durable-choice.jsonl` | 5 | 23 |
| `tor-admission-regression.jsonl` | 4 | 29 |
| `tor-capture-reader.jsonl` | 3 | 11 |

The separate existing cancelled-preparation case at that snapshot exposed the
lost connection-cancellation error identity. The final initial-expiry repair
was then executed at local snapshot
`f89a1caf387308ce6026e54a32ac1ac97fbf0553`: **15 passing verdicts across four
roots**, with no skips. These were the new prepared-owner cause/cleanup root
and the existing Speed Lab cancelled/replaced preparation, start-failure/success
handoff, and changed-private-directory roots. The receipt
`linux-tor-prelaunch-race.log` has SHA256
`6312282b5389e0a8e24bffcb56e375cf3b0c2d17f4982fc5a9b5c753e50536f3`.

The same `f89a1caf` snapshot also cross-compiled both `cmd/client` and
`internal/windowscontroller` test executables for Windows AMD64 and ARM64;
the resulting PE machine headers matched their targets. This is compilation
evidence for that integrated source, with Windows execution tracked separately.

Byte comparisons between those snapshots covered Tor source/tests,
`bundled_native.go`, `external_profile_connect.go` and Speed Lab source/tests.
Only `tor_bridge_prelaunch.go` and the added cause/cleanup test differed in that
comparison. The final guard and related prelaunch tests also matched local
fix `f519b5d2c8e12f5dd07df6417423770204d0a8b2` byte for byte. This bounds the
component comparison; it does not mean the complete earlier matrix ran again
at the later snapshot or that the two complete trees are identical.

On `f519b5d2`, the pure Python
[native shipping audit](../../deploy/test_native_shipping_audit_gates.py)
passed all **34** methods and the
[Windows release contracts](../../deploy/test_windows_release_gates.py)
passed all **22**. The new prelaunch root is already selected exactly once by
the mandatory `^TestDesktopTor` partition on both Windows architectures in
the native-contract and authoritative release workflows. No optional gate or
production timeout relaxation was added.

The isolated Tor publication was additionally checked on top of the preserved
`fb924d48` branch state: all **32** shipping-audit methods and **43** Tor
circumvention audit methods passed. The two extra shipping-audit methods in
the integrated snapshot belong to the separate protected imported-path unit.
Thirty-three changed files matched the final integrated snapshot byte for byte.
The contextual Tor callback changes in the two shared Speed Lab files also
matched; those files contain additional imported-host work in that snapshot.
The two Python audit files retain the corresponding independent unit scopes.

## Acceptance boundary

These tests establish executable caller, ownership, private-file and fixture
process behavior. Controlled network observers do not prove a real Tor circuit.
Local physical-network capture still encounters the recorded Netlink permission
denial; affected physical cases remain failed evidence, not passing or skipped
acceptance. Windows kernel execution, device and off-LAN networking, current
full-release publication, signing and private deployment require their own
evidence. None is inferred from this focused controller record.
